Verified industry-facing official events are regulatory, not commercial expos: OMC social-equity workshops (one per county + Wilmington) and Division of Revenue tax workshops (New Castle, Kent, Sussex) held during implementation (State News 11 Feb 2025, official); Marijuana Control Act Oversight Committee / Appeals Commission public meetings via the Delaware public meeting calendar (State News, official). Commercial event listings found by search were for other states / Delaware County (OH/PA) or non-cannabis conferences — excluded per the same-name-place warning. Do not list any expo as “Delaware” without an organiser page showing a Delaware (state) venue/date in Run 2.

PART 7

Hemp licences

7.1 Delaware runs its own USDA-approved programme

“On January 27, 2020, USDA approved the State of Delaware Plan for a Domestic Hemp Production Program. With this approval, Delaware assumes the primary regulatory responsibility for hemp production within its jurisdiction.”

Source: Dept. of Agriculture — agriculture.delaware.gov OFFICIAL. USDA's list (updated 17 Feb 2026) still lists Delaware as “DE Approved Plan” — not a USDA direct-licence state: ams.usda.gov. History: SB 266 (signed 28 Aug 2018) allowed hemp only for agricultural/academic research in 2019; emergency Reg. 805 took effect Dec 2019; final order adopted 11 Mar 2021 (24 DE Reg. 861).

No hemp retailer licence exists in Reg. 805. The programme licences producers, processors and handlers only. Retail sale of compliant hemp/CBD is not licensed under 805 in the text read; any retailer category would have to come from another law — none found UNAVAILABLE.

7.2 Licence types — 3 DE Admin. Code 805, §§805-4.0 / 805-5.0

Licence / registrationWhat it allowsFeesKey requirements / validity
Producer (grower)Grow, cultivate or distribute hemp, including transplants, seedlings or clones — any number of plants, any purpose including personal use. Must oversee seed cleaning, consulting, planting, harvesting and storing on their acreage (§805-4.2.4); notify landowner if not the owner.Application + renewal $300 / 3 years; Growing Site Registration $500/site/year; modification $500/siteAge 18+. Applications/renewals 1 Nov–1 Feb; site registration due by 1 Feb; licence valid to 31 Dec of the 3rd year after issue. GPS site map, State + Federal Criminal History Reports for key participants; annual report by 1 Dec; FSA acreage report within 15 days of planting; 1 regulatory sample per lot within 15 days of harvest.
ProcessorProcesses that convert raw hemp material in any way other than testing — includes producers who dry down or package their own crop. All materials/final products must be within acceptable hemp THC levels (§4.3).Application + renewal $300 / 3 years; Facility Registration $1,000/facility/year; modification $1,000/facilityApplications/renewals by 31 Oct; certificate valid to 31 Dec of 3rd year; facility registrations annually by 31 Oct. Criminal History Report for key participants (direct/indirect financial interest).
HandlerTransport or store hemp or hemp plant parts prior to delivery for processing, including seed. Licensed producers and registered processors do not need a handler licence.$100 annuallyAge 18+. Annual applications/renewals; licence valid to 31 Dec of issue year. Criminal History Report required.
Higher-education (research)Delaware institution of higher education producing/studying hemp must be licensed (§4.8). Over-limit crop grown for research and not entering commerce is not noncompliant if disposed of within a reasonable timeframe after research concludes (§4.8.2).Sample/testing fees always; producer/handler/processor fees only if participating beyond research§4.8–4.8.2
Nursery licence (adjacent)Required if a Licensed Producer sells viable plants/plant parts; under 3 Del.C. Ch. 13, not Reg. 805 itself. Also §805-8.5 nursery-law compliance.FreeVia Dept. of Agriculture
Hemp retailerNone in Reg. 805 — no retailer application, fee or validity period appears in §§805-4.0/5.0 or on the Dept. programme page. UNAVAILABLE — DO NOT INVENT ONE
Hemp testing lab (state licence)None in Reg. 805. Compliance samples are collected by a Dept.-designated sampling agent and sent to an external laboratory (§6.1.1). Sampling/testing fee $350 per sample (§5.3). Producer's own samples are at their own expense; only the Dept.-collected sample counts for regulatory purposes.

Common rules: Contact changes within 10 days; material falsification = ineligible (§4.1). Licences/registrations “may not be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged, or otherwise disposed of” (§805-8.7). Unlicensed production/processing/handling: civil penalty ≤$500/offence + immediate application (§8.8.1). Three negligent violations in 5 years = revocation + 5-year ineligibility; immediate revocation for a controlled-substance felony, materially false statement, or over-limit growing/handling/processing with culpable mental state above negligence (§4.7.3). Pesticide users must comply with Delaware Pesticide Laws, 3 Del.C. Ch. 12 (§8.6). Info: Dept. hemp programme; questions to DDA_HempProgram@delaware.gov.

“Hemp is the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant… with the federally defined delta-9 THC concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis, or the THC concentration for hemp defined in 7 U.S.C. sec 5940, whichever is greater.” — Dept. programme page (verbatim)

Regulatory samples test “post decarboxylated delta(9)-Tetrahydrocannabinol concentration” (total-THC style, consistent with Run 1). Lots >0.3% post-decarboxylated delta-9 require destruction on-site within 72 hours of notification (§805-8.1) or disposal (§805-8.2); Reg. 805 contains no remediation — the ≤0.5% / reasonable-efforts provision (§805-8.4.1.1) is a safe harbour against a negligent-violation finding only, and destruction/disposal is still required.

Scale (Dept. table): Registered producers 11 (2022), 8 (2023), 8 (2024), 8 (2025); producers who produced a crop 7, 4, 2, 0; outdoor acres planted 8.45, 1.75, 1.25, 0; 2025 intended use 100% CBD. The programme is very small and, in 2025, produced no crop on the Dept.'s own figures.

7.3 Registry — FOIA exclusion verified; no public registry exists

“Any records of the Department that could provide the name or location of a growing site or producer are excluded from the public record under 29 Del.C. § 10002(l) (1), (2), and (17) a. The Department may report information regarding licensees to law enforcement.” — §805-4.1 (verbatim)

This confirms Run 1: there is no public hemp registry to link. Nuance VERIFY: the Dept. page says files “may be subject to disclosure under FOIA” generally — that sentence does not override the specific §805-4.1 name/location exclusion; aggregate counts are published, individual names/locations are excluded. USDA programme contact for Delaware: DDA_HempProgram@delaware.gov; 302-698-4577 — ams.usda.gov.

7.4 Testing labs / DEA registration under the USDA delay

USDA (23 Dec 2025): enforcement of the DEA-registered-lab hemp testing requirement is delayed — non-DEA-registered labs may test until 31 Dec 2026 due to inadequate DEA-registered capacity; all other regulatory requirements still apply. Sources: ams.usda.gov; lab information page. Delaware position: UNAVAILABLE — no Dept. statement, Reg. 805 amendment, or Delaware DEA-lab list addressing the delay was found. Do not assume Delaware labs are DEA-registered and do not name a Delaware hemp lab — none was found in an official source. No Delaware official response to PL 119-37 / PL 119-103 was found in Run 1 or Run 2 UNAVAILABLE — CARRIED FORWARD.

9.3 Ballot history and polling

Delaware has NO statewide citizen initiative — confirmed. Ballotpedia: “Delaware is one of 24 states with no form of citizen-initiated ballot measures” and “the only state that does not require voter approval for state constitutional amendments.” Only five statewide ballot measures were decided 1850–2025 (fewest of any state), none a citizen-initiated cannabis measure. Sources: ballotpedia.org; Ballotpedia News. Every Delaware cannabis change has therefore come through the General Assembly (plus veto/override/non-signature), never a ballot measure. No official Delaware-government source stating “no citizen initiative” in those words was opened — official-source confirmation VERIFY if a Delaware Constitution citation is required.

Polling (all secondary): University of Delaware, 10–22 Sept 2014, n=902 adults: 56% support legalisation, 39% opposed — SECONDARY SOURCES ONLY — VERIFY (original UD release not opened). University of Delaware, 2018: 61% of voters support legalising (cited in Senate Democrats release, 12 May 2022; no sample/margin in excerpt) — SECONDARY ONLY. No 2024–2026 Delaware-specific cannabis poll with pollster/date/sample was found UNAVAILABLE.

PART 10

Tribal and interstate

10.1 Tribes in Delaware

Federally recognised tribes headquartered in Delaware: NONE. No federally recognised tribe has a reservation/trust land base inside the state or is headquartered in Delaware on the federal list. Federally recognised tribes with historic Delaware (Lenape) ties are headquartered elsewhere — the Delaware Nation (Anadarko, OK) and the Delaware Tribe of Indians (Bartlesville, OK), plus Canadian Delaware communities. Federal-list confirmation VERIFY AGAINST FEDERAL REGISTER / BIA LIST IF FILING-GRADE CITATION NEEDED; the “none in Delaware” conclusion matches the official DNREC text, which names only two state-recognised tribes.

State-recognised tribes — confirmed from DNREC (official): “Today, the state of Delaware recognizes two tribes: the Lenape and the Nanticoke.” Delaware acknowledged the Nanticoke by 1881 and recognised the Nanticoke Indian Association as a nonprofit in 1922; in 2016 legislation signed by Gov. Markell recognised the Nanticoke and the Lenape as tribes with formal governing bodies “carrying out and exercising substantial governmental duties and powers.” Dennis Coker is chief of the Lenape Indian Tribe of Delaware. Source: dnrec.delaware.gov OFFICIAL. Full names: Lenape Indian Tribe of Delaware and Nanticoke Indian Association, Inc. (“Nanticoke Indian Tribe” is the same entity — a name variant, not a third tribe). The exact 2016 chapter/section number was not opened VERIFY TITLE 29 BEFORE CITING A SECTION.

Dispute note (reported, not hidden): The federally recognised Delaware Nation and Delaware Tribe of Indians do not acknowledge the Delaware state-recognised groups as Lenape governments and have described state-recognised Lenape groups as “corporations posing as Indigenous nations” (2023 Delaware Nation report; Delaware Tribe resolution — secondary). This does not change Delaware state-law recognition, but any “tribal cannabis” claim by a state-recognised group would face a legitimacy challenge from the federally recognised Delaware tribes.

Do any Delaware tribes operate cannabis/hemp businesses? NO evidence found. No state-recognised tribe was found operating a licensed marijuana establishment, compassion centre, or hemp licence in any official OMC, Dept. of Agriculture, or tribal source searched; no tribal-state cannabis compact, agreement, or dispute was found — UNAVAILABLE AS A POSITIVE; NEGATIVE SEARCH RESULT. Because neither tribe is federally recognised and neither holds trust land in Delaware, there is no tribal-sovereignty basis for a separate tribal cannabis programme: any tribal-entity business would need an ordinary OMC licence under 4 Del.C. Ch. 13 (and a Dept. licence under Reg. 805 for hemp) on the same terms as any other applicant. No tribal set-aside or licence category exists in Ch. 13 or Reg. 5001 as read; the only tribal reference in 5001 is evidentiary — a “tribal enrollment card issued by the governing authority of a federally recognized Indian tribe” counts as valid photo ID for retail purchase (§6.5.3.6.5), which a Delaware state-recognised tribe's card would not satisfy.

10.2 Interstate commerce

“No. Marijuana remains illegal under federal law, and you may not carry or transport marijuana over state lines. It remains illegal to leave (or enter) Delaware with any marijuana products – medical or adult-use (non-medical) products. Mailing marijuana in or out of the State is also illegal.” — OMC FAQ (verbatim)

No Delaware interstate cannabis commerce law, compact, or agreement was found. Nothing in 4 Del.C. Ch. 13 or Reg. 5001 as read authorises import/export, an interstate compact, or adult-use reciprocity. Medical reciprocity is the sole cross-border consumer mechanism, and it is one-way in-state purchase, not interstate commerce: out-of-state medical patients may purchase from Delaware dispensaries via a visiting-patient application (QR code at the dispensary; upload home-state licence + medical card; Delaware out-of-state ID card expiring with the home card), while “Only Delaware residents can apply for the Delaware Medical Marijuana Program” (OMC FAQ). Visiting patients may not take Delaware product home across a state line. Hemp may move interstate under the 2018 Farm Bill framework, but Delaware creates no interstate hemp-commerce agreement; in-state transport/storage is via the handler licence (Part 7) — no Delaware hemp compact found UNAVAILABLE. HB 395 (filed 30 Apr 2026, secondary) would move intoxicating hemp products into licensed marijuana stores with a 0.4 mg total-THC/container threshold — a proposed bill, NOT law, and intrastate, not an interstate-commerce law.

Operative status under the Federal Baseline (given): Adult-use remains Schedule I → no interstate adult-use commerce is operative or authorised. State-licensed medical in Schedule III (from 28 Apr 2026) does not by itself authorise interstate medical commerce; Delaware's FAQ transport ban remains the operative consumer rule and no Delaware law triggered by the scheduling change was found. For hemp, the PL 119-37 §781 redefinition (limited effect 12 Nov 2026; full definition after 11 Dec 2026 per PL 119-103 §2019) will change what counts as hemp interstate, but no Delaware implementing law, trigger, or official response was found UNAVAILABLE — until Delaware amends 3 Del.C. Ch. 28 / Reg. 805, its own 0.3% post-decarboxylation standard remains the state rule. Delaware's financial-services protection is intrastate only: 16 Del.C. §4764A(d) (HB 355, 84 Del. Laws c. 485) — a provider of deposits, credit, funds-transfer, cash transport or accounting services in compliance with Delaware law to a licensed establishment “is not subject to criminal prosecution under Delaware law solely by virtue of providing any such service.”

PART 11

Operations rules

All section numbers are 4 DE Admin. Code 5001 unless stated as 4 Del.C. Official text: regulations.delaware.gov — accessed 24 Oct 2026.

11.1 Testing

Required tests — §11.2: All marijuana/products must be tested by a licensed testing facility under 4 Del.C. §1333 before transfer to a retail store (§11.1.1); the transferring establishment is responsible for ensuring every required test is done. The Commissioner may temporarily waive an analyte if public health/safety is not at risk and may require immediate mandatory testing for any other suspected hazard analyte.

  • Flower (pre-rolls, shake, trim, kief, all forms) — §11.2.1: cannabinoid profile (THCV, CBD, CBC, delta-8 THC, delta-9 THC, CBG, CBN); foreign matter; microbiological (aerobic bacteria, molds, yeast, coliform, E. coli); heavy metals (lead, mercury, arsenic, cadmium); terpenes; pesticides; total water activity.
  • Concentrates — §11.2.2: all flower tests plus residual solvents (butane, methanol, isopropanol, acetone, benzene, toluene, xylenes, chlorinated solvents, ethanol, and the full §11.2.2 list).
  • Edibles — §11.2.3: flower + concentrate tests plus validation of stated cannabinoid content.
  • Inhalable/vaporisable non-flower — §11.2.4: flower + concentrate tests plus MCT, PEG, vegetable glycerin, vitamin E acetate, diacetyl, squalene.

Failure thresholds — §11.3: foreign matter ≥1%; total coliform if detected; E. coli if detected; molds ≥400 cfu/g; yeast ≥400 cfu/g; lead ≥0.5 ppm; mercury ≥0.1 ppm; arsenic ≥0.2 ppm; cadmium ≥0.2 ppm; pesticides ≥10 ppb; residual solvents if detected (ethanol threshold ≥10% concentration). Full text: Cornell — 5001-11.0.

Sampling — §11.4: Commissioner publishes a Best Practices Guide for Sample Collection. Samples are randomly selected by the establishment under Division supervision for each batch; emergency self-sampling only if the Commissioner temporarily allows it, in plain view of cameras. Sample sizes ≈0.25% of batch weight (max flower batch 10 lb; min sample 3 g; ≤5 lb → 3 g, 5.1–10 lb → 11 g; concentrates ≤1 lb → 1.5 g up to 4.1–5 lb → 5 g; retail units: up to 500 → 10 units, 501–1,000 → 20, 1,001–3,000 → 35, 3,001–10,000 → 55, >10,001 → 80 (corrected — the earlier table was wrong in every row)). Edibles must be submitted as individual, non-packaged units (§11.4.4); samples generally packaged per §9.0 (§11.4.5).

Failed tests / retest / remediation / recalls — §§11.5–11.6, 14.2.6: Labs must report a failure to the Commissioner and the submitting establishment within 48 hours (§11.5.3); a passing batch is entered as “pass” in seed-to-sale and released for retail transfer. R&D tests are not reportable with advance R&D notice (§11.5.5). Failed batches may be retested with or without remediation; edibles may be retested 1 time — fail twice = destroy per §12.0. Without remediation: 2 samples to the same lab, both must pass every failed analyte; flower/trim that fails retest may go to a licensed manufacturer for extraction with Commissioner approval (§11.6.6); concentrates/products that fail retest may not be remediated or retested and must be destroyed. Remediation is NOT available for pesticide failures (§11.6.5). Approved remediation includes pascalization, ozone, non-ionising and ionising radiation (ionising exposure must be labelled per §9.2). Failed batches may not be blended with untested/passing batches. Action must be recorded within 30 days of failed-test notice or the Commissioner may order destruction (§11.6.11). Recalls (§14.2.6): the Commissioner may require recall where product contains an unauthorised pesticide, failed a mandatory test unmitigated, is unfit for human use, is improperly packaged/labelled, was not made by a licensed establishment, or poses a public-health/safety risk — with public notice, seed-to-sale lock, and purchaser notification. No specific Delaware recall event (named product/batch/date) was found UNAVAILABLE FOR AN ACTUAL RECALL INSTANCE.

Certified/accredited labs — official list: NONE FOUND UNAVAILABLE. §6.6.2.1 requires a testing facility to operate to ISO 17025 as confirmed by accreditation from an ILAC-recognised body, maintaining accreditation at all times for at least 1 required analyte/technology (§6.6.13.7). Director: doctorate + 2 yrs analytical lab experience, OR master's + 4 yrs, OR bachelor's + 5 yrs (§6.6.6.1). Labs may hold no direct/indirect financial interest in a cultivation, manufacturing or retail licensee (§6.6.13.1), may accept no gifts from them, and must keep results confidential except to the submitter, authorised law enforcement, CDC or the Commissioner. Labs may also test private-citizen / cardholder / 21+ consumer samples at the citizen's cost, outside the reporting rules (§6.6.5). No OMC-published list naming the licensed testing facilities was found — the 5 pool licences are a count, not a directory. A secondary claim of a contract with “High Tide Lab Company” is SECONDARY ONLY — NOT ADOPTED; it conflicts with the licensed-independent-lab model in §6.6. Seed-to-sale: 5001 §2.0 defines the system generically with no vendor name — “BioTrack” remains SECONDARY-ONLY — VERIFY; do not present it as the official system name.

11.2 Licence rules

Renewal: Licences are biennial (24 months). 5001 §5.1.1: apply “on or before the first day of the third month preceding the biennial expiration date” (~3 months before expiry). OMC FAQ's “at least 30 days before expiration” is a simplification — follow §5.1.1 for filing VERIFY FOR FILING. Renewal requires the form, a §1347 criminal background check (fingerprinting + state/federal; for entities, officers/directors/employees/owners ≥10%), ownership/control documentation, and the renewal fee; the Commissioner/Division may fully inspect first, and a deficient application may be cured by a correction plan + amended application. Failure to renew = “must cease operations at all premises and may not sell or give away marijuana” (§5.1.5); reinstatement is available on fee + approved application. Microbusiness licensees may not request a tier increase; cultivation may increase 1 tier per renewal period on an updated safety/security/diversion plan (4 Del.C. §1334(g)). Conversion licences: 24-month expiry, none after 1 Nov 2024; before expiry the holder may renew as an open licence for a $10,000 renewal fee (4 Del.C. §1343A(b)–(d), via FindLaw republication VERIFY AGAINST DELCODE).

“The rights conferred by a license may be transferred by the Commissioner to any representative designated by the person to whom or on behalf of whom the license was originally granted, if such representative is a person approved by the Commissioner. In the case of death of a licensee, the Commissioner may transfer the license to a qualified person recommended by the executor or administrator…” — 4 Del.C. §1366(a) (verbatim)

Transfers / ownership: §1366(b): inventory and payments on an approved transfer are handled directly transferor↔transferee. 5001 §5.2.2: the Commissioner may deny a transfer if the transferee fails Title 4/regulation requirements, has a moral-turpitude conviction/nolo plea, is in tax arrears anywhere, or the transfer violates law or other good cause exists; an unlawful transfer may be fined, voided, or the licence cancelled (§5.2.4). All material changes require the Commissioner's express approval (§5.4); before a conditional licence issues, no transfer of controlling interest/control, or of an interest defeating social-equity status (§4.5.7.2–4.5.7.4). Social-equity lock-up: “A social equity license may not be transferred to any person who does not meet the criteria for issuance of a social equity license until at least three (3) years after the licensee is awarded an active social equity license” (§1366(c)); 5001 §6.1.1 says “since the social equity license was initially awarded” — slight trigger-wording difference VERIFY WHICH CONTROLS.

Ownership cap — §4.1.2: no applicant may own/operate “more than 1 marijuana establishment of the same license type in a single county.” Different licence types in the same county are allowed; the cap “does not apply to converted medical licenses.” Testing-lab applicants are further restricted from applying for any other licence type (consistent with §6.6.13.1).

Conditional → active (§§4.6–4.8; 4 Del.C. §§1345–1346): Selected applicants have 30 days to complete the supplemental application + pay fees (extendable on good-faith effort). Supplemental requirements include background checks for every owner/officer/director/employee, a comprehensive financial background investigation, Certificates of Tax Clearance, 3-year litigation and 10-year licence-denial/suspension/revocation disclosures, and an inter-jurisdiction data-sharing waiver. A conditional licensee “must identify a physical location… and become operational within 18 months” (§4.6.1); extension only on demonstrated good-faith efforts, otherwise the Commissioner “must rescind” (§4.6.2). Termination grounds (§4.7) include moral-turpitude conviction, fraud, ineligibility, material non-disclosure, incomplete supplemental application, failure to convert, regulation violation, loss of Corporations good standing, and tax arrears. Active conversion (§4.8) requires approved safety/security/theft-diversion/operations plans, background-check costs paid, a labour-peace attestation, a form demonstrating compliance with all local laws (zoning, fire, building), demonstrated legal control of the site, and a completed full inspection.

Relocation — §5.3: “A marijuana establishment may not change the premises where the license is to be used without approval of the Commissioner” (application + fee). A Sept 2025 secondary report that OMC began allowing inter-county transfers is SECONDARY ONLY — VERIFY; the regulation itself requires only Commissioner approval + fee.

Surrender / revocation: §1363(a): cancellation or accepted surrender “entails the loss of the privilege conferred by the license” and seizure of marijuana/products in the licensee's possession (except solely by reason of death); notice by affixing to the premises door, leaving with the holder or an adult family member, or as the Commissioner judges will give notice; cancellation does not bar criminal proceedings, and seizure regulations must preserve (not destroy) product. §1361: the Commissioner may suspend, cancel (if repeated/continuous or egregious), or fine for violations including Ch. 13/regulation violations, false representation, failure to “maintain an acceptable bond, if a bond is required” (§1361(c)(3)), disorderly/unsanitary premises or impure product, sale to minors contrary to §1312, non-compliant product, deceptive seals/labels, and felony or marijuana-law conviction. 5001 §14.2.4–14.2.5 adds cancellation for repeated/continuous violations and corrective-action requirements. OMC FAQ: “licenses can be revoked if ownership requirement rules are violated, or if there are serious violations of regulations or law.”

Insurance / bonding: No general commercial-liability dollar minimum and no surety-bond dollar amount for ordinary marijuana licences was found in 5001 §§3–7 or the Code sections read — do not cite a Delaware cannabis bond/insurance dollar figure UNAVAILABLE. What exists: transport vehicles “must be insured at or above the legal requirements in Delaware” (§7.6.4.1), and §1361(c)(3) contemplates a bond “if a bond is required” without stating an amount. A $25,000 bond figure in search results belongs to a Title 5 banking licence and must not be imported.

11.3 Cultivation: plant counts, canopy, indoor/outdoor/greenhouse

“No marijuana cultivation facility license may be issued for an indoor facility with a cannabis plant grow canopy area exceeding 12,500 square feet or for an outdoor facility with a cannabis plant grow canopy area exceeding 7.5 acres unless additional tiers are created by the Commissioner under subsection (h).” — 4 Del.C. §1334(f) (verbatim substance)

Canopy is the control, not a plant count. §1334(h): the Commissioner may create additional tiers any time after 1 Aug 2025 if demand requires. Tier fees in the DelCode text: indoor 7,501–10,000 sq ft / outdoor 2.6–5 acres = $7,500; indoor ≥10,001 sq ft / outdoor ≥5.1 acres = $10,000 (biennial structure). Secondary tier summary (consistent): Tier 1 ≤2,500 sq ft; Tier 2 2,501–7,500; Tier 3 7,501–10,000; Tier 4 10,001–12,500. Microbusiness: indoor canopy ≤2,500 sq ft OR outdoor ≤1 acre; ≤10 employees (§1340; OMC FAQ). Former microbusiness conditions in §1340(1) and (4) (including any “1,000 plants monthly” figure) are [Repealed] and must not be presented as current requirements. No general plant-count cap was found UNAVAILABLE AS A CAP — canopy + tier is the operative limit. Whether a greenhouse counts as “indoor” or “outdoor” is not defined in the excerpts read VERIFY WITH OMC BEFORE SITING (Newark's MI district imposes indoor-only cultivation locally — a local rule, not a state rule). Seed-to-sale tracking from “seed or immature plant to sale” is required (§1334(c)); cultivators must provide samples to a licensed testing facility and keep records (§1334(d)); no consumption on cultivation premises (§1334(e)).

11.4 Security: cameras, alarms, storage, transport

  • Storage (§6.3.1): all usable plants, marijuana and products “stored in a locked and secured area.”
  • Access (§6.3.2.1): outside access closely monitored, limited to primary entry points; marijuana areas limited to authorised personnel; visitors/repair workers supervised; commercial-grade locks on all perimeter doors; all external entrances and perimeter windows lockable and in good condition.
  • Alarm (§6.3.2.2): fully operational; immediate alert to a licensed third-party security company on breach; audible alarm remotely disableable by the security company; automatic/electronic notification to public-safety personnel on loss of electrical backup; tamper protection for computers/records; maintenance inspection + test at intervals of no more than 30 days.
  • Lighting (§6.3.2.3): perimeter entry points lit so observers can see and cameras can record within 10 feet of the gate/entry.
  • Video (§6.3.2.4): permanently-affixed cameras viewing every area where marijuana is cultivated, manufactured, stored, prepared for transfer/sale, or where test samples are collected/prepared/sealed. Minimum 720p; continuous 24-hour recording OR motion-activated at ≥15 fps; NIST-standard time/date display; secured storage; footage held 90 days routine, up to 36 months if of significance; Commissioner 24/7 access via secure internet (§6.3.2.4.3).
  • Failure (§6.3.2.5): if alarm/video failure is expected to exceed 8 hours: notify the Commissioner within 24 hours; provide approved alternative security OR close affected addresses until restored; keep documentation ≥24 months.
  • Theft/diversion (§6.3.3): report to Commissioner + law enforcement within 1 business day; investigation complete within 30 business days; amend SOPs if directed. Monthly inventories (§6.4.1); discrepancies notified within 24 hours. Visitor logs retained 1 year. Employee ID badges (Commissioner-issued, fingerprint + criminal-history checked, 2-year renewal) visible at all times; lost/stolen badge notified within 24 hours; termination within 48 hours (§6.4.4).
  • Transport (§7.6): only between marijuana establishments, only with a seed-to-sale transport manifest, only in an enclosed locked vehicle area; shielded from public view; no removal until destination and no transfer/storage at unlicensed premises. Stops only for legally required meals/rest, refuelling, emergencies (prompt Commissioner notice), or multiple licensed deliveries; badged personnel stay with the vehicle. Vehicles insured at/above Delaware legal requirements, with a functional alarm and GPS tracking; only badged personnel aboard; Commissioner/Division inspection on request. The receiver verifies condition/quantity against the manifest and signs the transporter's copy.
  • Retail (§6.5.7): retail area physically separated from and locked against the waiting area (first area entered; no marijuana/products/accessories; reception verifies 21+ or medical card photo ID; attended at all times). Display not visible from a public right-of-way; locked display case, employee assistance only; potency shown next to product name; an employee must remain with a customer inspecting a display product; a display product removed from packaging may not then be sold/consumed (§6.5.5).

11.5 Waste disposal

General waste (§12.1): non-marijuana/medical/hazardous waste in tightly covered, leak-proof, rodent/animal-inaccessible dumpsters/load packers on concrete slabs (preferably graded to a drain, water supply nearby); disposal only in DNREC-approved sanitary landfills; secured against unauthorised access to marijuana waste. Source: Cornell — 5001-12.0.

“All marijuana waste must be rendered unusable before leaving a marijuana establishment by grinding and incorporating the marijuana waste with non-consumable solid wastes such that the resulting mixture is no greater than 50% marijuana waste.” — §12.2 (verbatim substance)

Non-consumable solids include paper, plastic, cardboard, food waste, soil, grease/other compostable waste, and other Commissioner-approved solids. On removal for destruction the establishment must immediately update seed-to-sale inventory (read the full §12.2 witnessing/documentation text before relying on procedure). Testing-facility waste (§6.6.12): medical waste per 7 DE Admin. Code 1302 Part 262 + hazardous waste per DNREC rules, in addition to §12.0. Hemp waste: over-limit lots require destruction/disposal; Reg. 805 §8.4.1.1 requires destruction/disposal of over-limit cannabis (with a reasonable-efforts / ≤0.5% safe harbour against a negligent-violation finding — destruction is still required). No hemp-waste rendering percentage was found UNAVAILABLE.

11.6 Environmental: water, pesticides, energy

Cultivation + manufacturing applicants must include “an environment and sustainability plan, including efforts it will take to minimize the environmental impact, and resource needs of its facilities… such as plans to minimize water usage, employing organic cultivation methods, and adoption of other sustainable business practices” (4 Del.C. §1331(3)). Food-handling facilities must comply with the Delaware Food Code (§6.2.2); premises must comply with the OSHA General Duty Clause, NFPA, International Building Code and International Fire Code (§6.2.3–6.2.4), including fire/smoke and carbon-monoxide detection. Pesticides: the operative marijuana limit is the testing threshold — pesticides ≥10 ppb = fail (§11.3.4) — plus recall for “unauthorized pesticide” (§14.2.6.1.1). No Delaware-published list of approved marijuana pesticides was found UNAVAILABLE — do not import another state's list. For hemp, Delaware Pesticide Laws (3 Del.C. Ch. 12) apply (Reg. 805 §8.6). Water / energy: no Delaware cannabis-specific water-right, water-use cap, energy cap, or renewable mandate was found in 5001 or Ch. 13 UNAVAILABLE — general DNREC permitting and local utility capacity apply; the sustainability plan is a plan requirement, not a numeric cap.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions

“Retail marijuana stores may not offer marijuana or marijuana products free of charge under any circumstance.” — §10.4.1 (verbatim) · “Marijuana or marijuana products may be discounted, provided that the discount is not made in conjunction with the retail sale of products other than marijuana.” — §10.4.2 (verbatim)

Retail stores are also prohibited from “Giving away marijuana, marijuana concentrates, and marijuana products for no remuneration” (§6.5.2.3), and statute bars discounting/free items bundled with a non-marijuana sale (§1382(f)). Net rule: standalone marijuana discounts ARE allowed; free marijuana is NEVER allowed at retail; a marijuana discount bundled with a non-marijuana sale is prohibited. The only “sampling” channels in 5001 are mandatory compliance testing (§11.4) and R&D (§11.7; §6.6.1.2) — R&D results cannot satisfy compliance testing. No “consumer sample” or “budtender sample” category exists in the text read. Advertising (§10.2): must not occur on radio/TV/broadcast/mass-transit/internet-or-app popups unless subscribers are 21+ or 21+ persons solicited it; no paid/unpaid in-person solicitation; no imitation of existing branded consumer products; no promotion of overconsumption; no depiction of actual consumption; no health/physical-benefit claims. Exterior signage may not contain a realistic marijuana-leaf depiction or exceed 75 sq ft (or the smaller local limit) (§10.2.3). Packaging/labelling may not use neon colours, fruit/human-or-animal shapes or celebrity likenesses (§9.7). Branding may not appeal to minors, imitate branded products, or use cartoons/caricatures/balloons/toys or minor-associated images/words (§10.3). Advertising must carry the §10.1.2 statements including “For use by adults 21+, KEEP OUT OF REACH OF CHILDREN!” and, for TV/radio/internet/app/social/print/event sponsorship, meet the 85% 21+ audience test with reliable current audience-composition data (§10.1.3). Medical promotions: §10.4 does not apply to promotions/deals for medical programme cardholders (§10.4.4).

11.8 Drug testing for licence holders / staff

No mandatory drug-testing programme for marijuana-establishment staff was found in 5001. What 5001 requires: a Commissioner-issued employment ID badge with fingerprinting + state/federal criminal-history checks (§6.4.4.2; denial for §1354(b)(4) offences, under-21, or assessed public-safety risk); an operating manual including “The marijuana establishment's alcohol and drug free workplace policies” (§6.4.2.8) — the employer must have a policy, but 5001 prescribes no panels, frequency, or consequences; and Commissioner-approved ID-recognition training for retail employees. Owners are criminal-history/financial-background checked, not drug-tested — no owner drug-testing requirement found UNAVAILABLE AS A REQUIREMENT. Outside 5001, Delaware has no general statute dictating private-employer drug-testing content (secondary summaries); the binding protection is for medical cardholders only under 16 Del.C. §4905A (Part 12) — no discrimination solely for cardholder status or a positive marijuana test unless the patient used, possessed, or was impaired on the employer's premises or during work hours. Adult-use users have no equivalent statutory protection in the sources read. Chance v. Kraft Heinz Foods Co. (implied private right of action under §4905A) is SECONDARY — VERIFY BEFORE LEGAL RELIANCE.

11.9 Research and university cannabis licences

No standalone “marijuana research licence” category exists in 5001 (§3.0 lists only retail, testing, cultivation, manufacturing — open, social-equity, microbusiness). Research runs through testing facilities receiving R&D samples (§6.6.1.2, §11.7) and cultivators/manufacturers providing samples “for testing and research purposes” (4 Del.C. §1334(d)). The only university research licence verified is hemp — Reg. 805 §4.8 (Part 7), with its over-limit research safe harbour. Secondary claims of a “Center for Marijuana Research at the University of Delaware,” DHSS research grants, and DE–PA–NY partnerships were not found in any official source and are not relied on VERIFY. No Delaware university marijuana-research programme was found UNAVAILABLE.

PART 12

Consumer and social rules

12.1 Paraphernalia, smoke shops, unlicensed sellers

Paraphernalia: HB 1 removed penalties for 21+ possession/use/display/purchase/transport of marijuana accessories with a personal-use quantity (decriminalised to a $100 civil violation by HB 39 in 2015, then legalised for adults in 2023). Retail stores may sell “marijuana accessories and non-consumable products that are not intended for human consumption, including clothing, storage containers, rolling papers, or other marijuana accessories” (§6.5.1.7).

“The only products the State of Delaware recognizes as safe are those purchased in licensed retail centers. Unregulated hemp derived products lack safety and purity testing and may contain dangerous compounds which have not been well studied.” — OMC FAQ (verbatim)

Enforcement: DATME “investigates and may arrest for violations of Title 4, Chapter 13 and Title 16, Chapter 49A, seize marijuana, including marijuana products and accessories that are manufactured, sold, kept, or transported in contravention of state law” (OMC FAQ; DelCode §403(4)). Delta-8 / intoxicating hemp: secondary sources uniformly describe delta-8, delta-10, HHC and similar converted/synthetic cannabinoids as prohibited outside licensed channels — but no Delaware statute or regulation text banning delta-8 by name was opened in Run 1 or Run 2 VERIFY — HB 270 CLAIM UNCONFIRMED. What is official: the OMC licensed-centres warning above; 5001 §6.5.2.4 (retail stores may not sell products containing synthetic cannabinoids); HB 373's lawful channel only for naturally-derived delta-9 hemp beverages; and HB 395 (proposed, not law) folding >0.4 mg products into the marijuana system. Report the position as: unlicensed intoxicating-hemp sales are treated as unlicensed marijuana activity subject to DATME seizure, but a freestanding “delta-8 ban” statute was not verified. Smoke shops: no Delaware smoke-shop licence, registration, or smoke-shop-specific rule was found in 5001, Reg. 805, or OMC pages UNAVAILABLE; no named Delaware smoke-shop enforcement action was found UNAVAILABLE.

12.2 THC driving limits and impairment testing

No numeric per se THC blood limit for lawful adult-use/medical use was found in Delaware law as read. Secondary summaries describe 21 Del.C. §4177 as having two prongs — (1) driving “while under the influence of any drug” (impairment) and (2) driving with “any trace of a controlled substance in [the] blood within four hours of driving” for unlawful drug use (the “zero-tolerance” prong secondary tables cite). The statute text itself was not opened in Run 2, so the prong structure is VERIFY AGAINST 21 DEL.C. §4177 BEFORE LEGAL RELIANCE, as are all secondary DUI penalty figures. Medical-patient protection: §4904A(a)(4) — a registered qualifying patient (or visiting patient) “shall not be considered to be under the influence of marijuana solely because of the presence of metabolites or components of marijuana that appear in insufficient concentration to cause impairment” — a metabolites-alone shield, not a THC-number shield. In practice (secondary): DelDOT warned in Aug 2025 that cannabis DUI remains illegal after sales began; enforcement relies on behavioural impairment, field sobriety / drug-recognition evaluation, and chemical testing (no THC breathalyser equivalent); implied-consent consequences apply to refusals. OMC FAQ prohibits consumption in a vehicle; the widely reported closed-container transport rule should be verified against 21 Del.C. / §4764A text before citation VERIFY.

12.3 Expungement and sealing

Delaware has mandatory/automatic expungement for low-level marijuana possession in several layers (all summaries secondary, consistent — session-law verification VERIFY): 2018 SB 197 — mandatory eligibility for a single conviction for ≤1 oz; 2019 SB 37 — single cannabis misdemeanour expungeable after 5 years, single felony after 7 years; 2021 Clean Slate laws — all misdemeanour cannabis-possession convictions eligible for mandatory expungement, drug felonies after 10 years, with the process automatic from mid-2024 (1 Aug 2024 per the Office of Defense Services summary) — though incomplete digitisation means records can be missed, so a petition remains the safer route for an individual. Separately, 16 Del.C. §4764(i): a person convicted of a single §4764(c) offence while under 21 may, on reaching 21, apply for expungement of the conviction + arrest indicia; the order “shall issue” on proof of age 21 unless the person failed to comply with the sentencing order or has another outstanding §4764 charge ($100 SBI administrative fee + civil filing fee). The Clean Slate mechanism sits in 11 Del.C. Ch. 43 Subch. VII (§§4371 et seq.) — check the session laws before filing use. A secondary claim that “HB 150 §4770” created legalisation-era mandatory expungement is removed: HB 150’s expungement provisions were removed during passage as duplicative of SB 111/SB 112 — do not cite HB 150/§4770 as an expungement authority. Run 1 link retained: 7% of total marijuana tax money received in the preceding month goes to the Justice Reinvestment Fund (§1386(a)(2)).

12.4 Consumption lounges and events

No consumption-lounge licence exists in Delaware. 5001 licence types are only retail, testing, cultivation, manufacturing (§3.0). Retail stores must “Prohibit the consumption of marijuana or marijuana products on the premises” (§6.5.3.2); cultivation facilities likewise (§1334(e)). Public consumption is prohibited (OMC FAQ: “Consuming marijuana in an area accessible to the public is illegal”; “Consuming marijuana in a vehicle is Illegal”). No cannabis-event / temporary-event licence, on-site consumption event permit, or festival-consumption exception was found in 5001 or Ch. 13 UNAVAILABLE — private adult consumption of lawfully possessed product on private property is not “licensed consumption,” and any event in an area accessible to the public falls under the public-consumption ban. Do not market a Delaware “consumption lounge” or “cannabis event licence” — neither exists in the law read. HB 373 beverages are for off-premises consumption only (no on-site drinking; no third-party/mail delivery; package-store online order for in-store pickup/curbside only).

12.5 Tourism and visitor purchase limits

Adult-use visitors: NO residency requirement in the law read — purchase is age-gated, not residency-gated. 5001 §6.5.3.6 requires verification by “a valid, government-issued identification” and lists as valid: a driver's licence “issued by any state within the United States, District of Columbia, or U.S. territory” (§6.5.3.6.1); a state ID card from any state; US military/government ID (incl. permanent-resident, alien-registration, consular cards); a passport/passport card; and a tribal enrollment card from a federally recognised tribe. An “any state” ID rule would be pointless if only Delaware residents could buy. Caveat: OMC's FAQ does not state “tourists may buy” in words — the conclusion rests on §6.5.3.6 + the absence of any residency clause in §1332/§6.5, i.e. an official-text inference, not an express OMC tourism statement VERIFY IF AN EXPRESS STATEMENT IS REQUIRED. Quantity: visitors get the same personal-use quantity as residents (1 oz leaf / 12 g concentrate / 750 mg delta-9 THC products per transaction/possession; §6.5.2.2). Tax: Delaware has no general sales tax, but the 15% marijuana tax (§1382) applies — visitors pay 15% + no sales tax; do not claim Delaware cannabis is “tax-free.” Hard limits: (i) no taking product out of Delaware (OMC FAQ transport ban applies equally, including into PA/NJ/MD); (ii) no public consumption, vehicle consumption, or home grow; (iii) no adult-use delivery to a hotel or out-of-state address — in-store, in person only (§6.5.2.9); (iv) visitors may not join the medical programme (Delaware residents only) but visiting medical patients can buy medical product via the visiting-patient card (Part 10) at medical quantities (3 oz / 14 days, 6 oz/month) with no marijuana tax.

12.6 Landlord / tenant rules

“No school or landlord may refuse to enroll or lease to, or otherwise penalize, a person solely for his or her status as a registered qualifying patient or a registered designated caregiver, unless failing to do so would cause the school or landlord to lose a monetary or licensing-related benefit under federal law or regulations.” — 16 Del.C. §4905A(a)(1) (republished text)

This protects status as a cardholder, not a right to smoke/use on the premises. No general adult-use tenant protection was found — adult-use consumers have no §4905A equivalent in Ch. 13 as read, so a landlord may prohibit smoking/use by lease (HOA/condo enforcement turns on the association's governing documents/nuisance provisions, secondary). Federally subsidised housing may prohibit marijuana regardless of state law (secondary VERIFY BEFORE ADVISING A PUBLIC-HOUSING TENANT). Home grow is prohibited for everyone, so no tenant has a right to grow on leased premises; a commercial tenant needs an OMC licence + landlord consent/site control (§4.8.5). A landlord leasing to a licensed establishment does not become a licensee, but the property becomes subject to OMC/Division inspection while a licensee/customer is on the premises (DelCode §403(5)). See also §1307 (Part 1): owners/occupiers may prohibit/regulate marijuana on their property, with the three listed limits on residential landlords.

12.7 Gun ownership conflicts

Federal baseline applied: 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) bars firearm/ammunition possession by a person “who is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance.” Under the given baseline, state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III (28 Apr 2026) while adult-use remains Schedule I. Do not overclaim: Schedule III status does not automatically remove the §922(g)(3) disability — §922(g)(3) turns on “unlawful user” status, and whether state-licensed Schedule III medical use counts as “lawful” use for §922(g)(3) / ATF Form 4473 purposes is a federal interpretation question the baseline does not resolve; no ATF guidance update addressing the split was found. Adult-use users remain squarely within the historic §922(g)(3) prohibition; medical users' post-split position is legally uncertain VERIFY — FEDERAL GUIDANCE REQUIRED. ATF's 2011 open letter (dealers have reasonable cause to believe state medical-card holders are unlawful users) has not been shown to have changed; secondary reports of Supreme Court review of §922(g)(3) in early 2026 have no outcome in this run UNAVAILABLE. Delaware position: UNAVAILABLE AS A CURRENT OFFICIAL GUIDANCE STATEMENT — no OMC, Attorney General, or State Police guidance on §922(g)(3) after split scheduling was found. HB 276 (2022) proposed patient firearm-transaction/NICS protections — a proposed bill, NOT enacted law as read, status unconfirmed VERIFY — DO NOT CITE AS LAW; secondary summaries of 11 Del.C. §§1448/1448B are likewise VERIFY BEFORE RELIANCE.

12.8 Pet / veterinary products

No Delaware veterinary-cannabis statute, veterinary-board rule, or pet-product licence was found UNAVAILABLE AS A DELAWARE-SPECIFIC RULE. By default: hemp-derived CBD pet products must meet the same hemp definition (≤0.3% delta-9 dry weight, post-decarboxylation crop testing) as human hemp products; marijuana-derived pet products would be marijuana products under Ch. 13/49A, saleable only via licensed channels, with no veterinary-prescription pathway in the law read. Secondary summaries (“permits the sale of CBD products specifically formulated for pets”; pet CBD “not explicitly addressed in state law”) are consistent with the unavailable finding. No Delaware rule authorising or prohibiting veterinarians from recommending/discussing CBD was found, and no FDA-approved veterinary CBD drug exists — do not advise that a veterinarian may “prescribe” marijuana/CBD in Delaware; no such pathway was verified. Intoxicating-THC pet products fall under the §12.1 unlicensed-marijuana enforcement position.

12.9 Medical cannabis in schools

General ban, with a caregiver-administration exception whose current scope is a live 2026 amendment question VERIFY. Baseline ban (16 Del.C. §4904A(a)(2), pre-2026 text): medical use is not authorised “(A) in a school bus; (B) on the grounds of any preschool or primary or secondary school; or (C) in any correctional facility” / DHSS-operated/funded health-care facility. OMC FAQ repeats the ban. A secondary Americans for Safe Access summary states Delaware “Allows caregivers to administer on school grounds and on buses. Nurses or school staff may not administer unless they are also the parent or legal guardian” — the reconciliation of that caregiver-administration provision with the §4904A ban was not resolved from primary text VERIFY AGAINST CURRENT §4904A. SB 226 (2026) amends §4904A to add “Except as provided in subsection (b) or subsection (c)” before the ban and adds a health-care-facility limb (secondary summary: signed 21 May 2026, terminally ill patients may use medical marijuana in health care facilities — see Part 0); its school-specific subsections (b)/(c) were not read in full and its school enactment/effective date was not confirmed from legis.delaware.gov VERIFY SB 226 ENROLLED TEXT BEFORE ADVISING ANY SCHOOL — DO NOT STATE STUDENTS MAY NOW USE AT SCHOOL ON ITS BASIS. School discipline overlay: 14 DE Admin. Code §612-3.0 prohibits drugs/paraphernalia in the school environment “unless medically necessary” and requires Ch. 47 substances in a student's possession to be turned over to police (Cornell-hosted text VERIFY AGAINST OFFICIAL ADMIN. CODE). The §4905A(a)(1) anti-discrimination rule (above) bars schools from penalising solely for patient/caregiver status unless a federal monetary/licensing benefit would be lost — the proviso behind restrictive school practice in many cases. Bottom line: smoking/vaping on school grounds remains prohibited in all sources; non-smoked caregiver administration is the only pathway described, and its post-SB 226 footing is unverified. No school-nurse administration programme was verified for Delaware.
